{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=R_{a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(I,J,K,L).

Figure 1.
Possible vertices in vertex models and their weights.

In other words, we define four types of vertices
Id,W^{a},W^{b},R
, which are determined by the arrangement of the parameters
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
. For example, the vertex of type
W^{a}
preserves the parameters
a,a^{\prime}
but swaps the parameters
b,b^{\prime}
between the lines. Note that in each of these situations the vertex weight vanishes unless
I+J=K+L
, we call this fact the
conservation law
.

The
weight
of a configuration of a vertex model is the product of the weights of all vertices. A
boundary condition
for a vertex model is an assignment of labels to the boundary edges. Given a vertex model and a boundary condition we define the corresponding
partition function
as the sum of the weights of all configurations satisfying the boundary condition, that is, configurations whose labels of the boundary edges coincide with the boundary condition. For example, both sides of the identities from Propositions
3.5
,
3.6
can be rewritten as partition functions in the following way :

(4.1)

Here to each boundary edge we assign a triple
(I;a,b)
, where
I
is the label of the boundary condition, and
(a,b)
are the corresponding edge parameters. To lighten the notation, instead of specifying the edge parameters of the internal edges we specify the types of the vertices,
W^{a},W^{b}
or
R
; the labels of the internal edges can be uniquely reconstructed from this information.

Remark 4.1
.

The vertex models described above are directly related to the representations from Section
3
: we can think about the edge with parameters
(a,b)
as of the representation
V^{\sqrt{a}}_{\sqrt{b}}
, and each vertex corresponds to one of the operators
V^{\sqrt{a_{1}}}_{\sqrt{b_{1}}}\otimes V^{\sqrt{a_{2}}}_{\sqrt{b_{2}}}\to V^{\sqrt{\tilde{a}_{1}}}_{\sqrt{\tilde{b}_{1}}}\otimes V^{\sqrt{\tilde{a}_{2}}}_{\sqrt{\tilde{b}_{2}}}
. Note that the whole partition function corresponds to a matrix coefficient of the isomorphism between two tensor products of the form
V^{\sqrt{a_{1}}}_{\sqrt{b_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes V^{\sqrt{a_{L}}}_{\sqrt{b_{L}}}
, with the parameters
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
determined by the parameters of the incoming and outgoing edges.

Remark 4.2
.

One can also write the usual Yang-Baxter equation
(1\otimes R)(R\otimes 1)(R\otimes 1)=(R\otimes 1)(1\otimes R)(R\otimes 1)
using vertex models:

Note that when
a_{1}=a_{2}=a_{3}
, the equation (
4.1
) becomes equivalent to the usual Yang-Baxter equation, while setting
a_{1}=a_{2},b_{2}=b_{3}
makes (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) equivalent.

4.2.
Row operators

For
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
define
V^{(N)}
as the vector space over
\Bbbk
with a basis consisting of the vectors
|A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{N}\rangle
enumerated by
N
-tuples
(A_{1},\dots,A_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}_{\geq 0}
. Equivalently, the same basis can be enumerated by partitions
\mu
of length at most
N
in the following way:

|\mu\rangle=|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2},\mu_{2}-\mu_{3},\dots,\mu_{N}\rangle;

we use both notations interchangeably. For
N<M
we have an embedding
V^{(N)}\subset V^{(M)}
which is defined by
|\mu\rangle\mapsto|\mu\rangle
where
l(\mu)\leq N<M
, or, equivalently,
|A_{1},\dots,A_{N}\rangle\mapsto|A_{1},\dots,A_{N},0,\dots,0\rangle
. Let
\langle\lambda|
,
\langle B_{1},\dots,B_{N}|
denote the vectors dual to the basis
\{|\mu\rangle\}_{l(\mu)\leq N}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{A}=(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
and
\mathcal{B}=(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\dots)
denote two infinite sequences of parameters. For variables
x,y
we define row-to-row transfer matrices
T^{b,N}_{I,L}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),T^{a,N}_{K,J}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):V^{(N)}\to V^{(N)}
by setting

(4.4)

T^{b,N}_{I,L}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):|J_{1},\dots,J_{N}\rangle\mapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K_{1},\dots,K_{N}:\\
L_{r}:=I+J_{[1,r]}-K_{[1,r]}\geq 0,\\
L_{N}=L\end{subarray}}\prod_{r=1}^{N}W^{b}_{a_{r+1},x,b_{r}}(L_{r-1},J_{r},K_{r},L_{r})|K_{1},\dots,K_{N}\rangle

(4.5)

T^{a,N}_{K,J}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):|I_{1},\dots,I_{N}\rangle\mapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},\dots,L_{N}:\\
J_{r}:=K+L_{[1,r]}-I_{[1,r]}\geq 0,\\
J_{N}=J\end{subarray}}\prod_{r=1}^{N}(y/b_{0})^{-I_{r}}W^{a}_{a_{r},y,b_{r}}(I_{r},J_{r},J_{r-1},L_{r})|L_{1},\dots,L_{N}\rangle,

where for a sequence
(A_{1},\dots,A_{N})
we set
A_{[1,0]}=0
,
A_{[1,r]}=A_{1}+\dots+A_{r}
for
r\geq 1
. The same definitions can be graphically represented using the following partition functions:

(4.6)

\langle K_{1},\dots,K_{N}|\,T^{b,N}_{I,L}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|J_{1},\dots,J_{N}\rangle=\hbox to235.85pt{\vbox to73.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.55272pt\lower-36.98717pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.85,0.85,0.85}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -35.43 0 L 109.84 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{131.56955pt}{3.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

Here we follow the same conventions as in (
4.1
), (
4.2
), and in the second partition function we use the vertices of type
W^{a}
rotated by
90^{\circ}
. Note that for each partition function above there exists at most one configuration with nonzero weight, and
L_{r},J_{r}
from (
4.4
), (
4.5
) respectively are the labels of the internal edges in these configurations.

In the following statement we summarize simple facts about the operators
T^{b,N}_{I,0}
and
T^{a,N}_{K,0}
:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\lambda,\mu
be partitions of length at most
N
.

(1) Duality: The operators
T^{a,N}_{K,0}
and
T^{b,N}_{K,0}
are dual in the following sense:

\langle\mu|\,T^{a,N}_{K,0}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle=(a_{0}/y)^{K}\frac{(y/b_{1};q)_{K}}{(q;q)_{K}}\frac{\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}{\psi_{\mu}(\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})}\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{K,0}(y^{-1}\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})\,|\mu\rangle,

where
\tau\mathcal{B}
denotes the shifted sequence and
\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is defined by

\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\prod_{r\geq 0}(b_{r}/a_{r})^{\lambda_{r+1}}\prod_{r\geq 1}\frac{(q;q)_{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}}{(a_{r}/b_{r};q)_{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}}.

(2) Polynomiality:
\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{I,L}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle
is a polynomial in
x
, and
\langle\mu|\,T^{a,N}_{K,J}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle
is a polynomial in
y^{-1}
. Moreover, both are rational functions in
q
regular at
q=0
.

(3) Interlacing:
\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=\langle\mu|\,T^{a,N}_{I,0}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle=0
unless
\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}=I
and
\mu\prec\lambda
, that is,

\lambda_{1}\geq\mu_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\dots.

In particular, for any
M>N
we have
T^{b,M}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,V^{(N)}\subset V^{(N+1)}
and
T^{a,M}_{K,0}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,V^{(N)}\subset V^{(N)}
.

(4) Stability: For any
M>N
we have

\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,M}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle,\qquad\langle\mu|\,T^{a,M}_{K,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle=\langle\mu|\,T^{a,N}_{K,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle.

Proof.

Part (1) follows at once from (
4.4
), (
4.5
) and the relation

W^{a}_{a_{r},y,b_{r}}(I_{r},J_{r},J_{r-1},L_{r})\\
=(a_{r}/b_{r})^{-J_{r}}(y/b_{r})^{L_{r}}\frac{(q;q)_{I_{r}}}{(a_{r}/b_{r};q)_{I_{r}}}\frac{(q;q)_{J_{r}}}{(y/b_{r+1};q)_{J_{r}}}\frac{(y/b_{r};q)_{J_{r-1}}}{(q;q)_{J_{r-1}}}\frac{(a_{r}/b_{r+1};q)_{L_{r}}}{(q;q)_{L_{r}}}W^{b}_{b_{r+1}^{-1},y^{-1},a_{r}^{-1}}(J_{r-1},L_{r},I_{r},J_{r}),

which is verified using (
3.3
) and (
3.4
). Part (2) is also immediate from (
3.3
), (
3.4
), see Remark
3.4
.

For the interlacing statement from part (3), by part (1) it is enough to prove it for
T^{b,N}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
. Set
J_{r}=\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}
,
K_{r}=\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}
and
L_{r}=I+J_{[1,r]}-K_{[1,r]}
. Recall from (
3.4
) that the weights
W^{b}_{a_{r+1},x,b_{r}}(L_{r-1},J_{r},K_{r},L_{r})
vanish unless
J_{r}\geq L_{r}\geq 0
. Hence, for
\langle K_{1},\dots,K_{N}|\,T^{b,N}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|J_{1},\dots,J_{N}\rangle
to be nonzero we must have
J_{r}\geq L_{r}\geq 0
for every
r
, and moreover
L_{N}=0
. Noting that
L_{r}=I+\mu_{1}-\mu_{r+1}-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{r+1}
and, in particular,
L_{N}=I+\mu_{1}-\lambda_{1}
, we get that
\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=0
unless
I=\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}\geq I+\mu_{1}-\mu_{r+1}-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{r+1}\geq 0
for every
r
. The last inequality is equivalent to
\mu_{r}\geq\lambda_{r+1}\geq\mu_{r+1}
for all
r=1,\dots,N
, and since
\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}=I\geq 0
the interlacing
\mu\prec\lambda
follows.

The second statement of part (3) follows immediately from the interlacing by noticing that
\mu\prec\lambda
implies
l(\mu)\leq l(\lambda)\leq l(\mu)+1
. Finally, part (4) follows from the fact that
W^{b}_{a,x,b}(0,0,0,0)=W^{a}_{a,y,b}(0,0,0,0)=1
, and so if
l(\lambda),l(\mu)\leq N<M
we have

\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,M}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=W^{b}_{a_{N+1},x,b_{N}}(0,0,0,0)\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,M-1}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,M-1}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle

and similarly for
T^{a,M}_{K,0}
.
∎

Let
V:=\bigcup_{N}V^{(N)}
be a vector space over
\Bbbk
with a basis
\{|\mu\rangle\}_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}
where we have no restrictions on the length of
\mu
. The natural embeddings
V^{(N)}\subset V
are given by identifying the vectors
|\mu\rangle
in
V
and
V^{(N)}
when
l(\mu)\leq N
. By parts (3) and (4) of Proposition
4.3
we can define operators
\mathbb{T}^{b}_{I}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),\mathbb{T}^{a}_{K}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):V\to V
by setting for
v\in V^{(N)}

\mathbb{T}^{b}_{I}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\ v=T^{b,N+1}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\ v,\qquad\mathbb{T}^{a}_{K}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\ v=T^{a,N}_{K,0}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\ v.

Using these operators, we can formally define

\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{r\geq 0}(x/b_{0})^{r}\frac{(a_{1}x^{-1};q)_{r}}{(q;q)_{r}}\mathbb{T}^{b}_{r}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),

\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{r\geq 0}\mathbb{T}^{a}_{r}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

More precisely,
\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is an operator
V\to V
such that

\langle\mu|\,\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle=\langle\mu|\,\mathbb{T}^{a}_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle,

which is well-defined since
\langle\mu|\,\mathbb{T}^{a}_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle\neq 0
only if
\mu\prec\lambda
, and there is a finite number of such
\mu
given a fixed
\lambda
. On the other hand,
\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is not a well-defined operator
V\to V
, since given a fixed
\mu
we have
\mathbb{T}^{b}_{r}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\neq 0
for infinitely many
r
in general. However, we can resolve this issue by considering
q
and
x
as formal variables: by Proposition
4.3
the matrix coefficients
\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{T}_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}^{b}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle
can be viewed as elements of the algebra of formal power series
\Bbbk[[x,q]]
, while the combined degree with respect to
x
and
q
of
(x/b_{0})^{r}\frac{(a_{1}x^{-1};q)_{r}}{(q;q)_{r}}
is at least
r
. Hence
\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a well-defined operator
V[[x,q]]\to V[[x,q]]
, where
V[[x,q]]
is the vector space of formal power series in
x,q
with coefficients in
V
.

The operators
\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
and
\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
are dual to each other in the following sense:

Proposition 4.4
.

For any
\lambda,\mu
we have

\langle\mu|\,\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle=\frac{\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}{\psi_{\mu}(\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})}\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}(y^{-1}\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})\,|\mu\rangle,

where
\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is defined by

\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\prod_{r\geq 0}(b_{r}/a_{r})^{\lambda_{r+1}}\prod_{r\geq 1}\frac{(q;q)_{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}}{(a_{r}/b_{r};q)_{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}}.

Proof.

Follows from Proposition
4.3
, part (1).
∎

4.3.
Exchange relations

Now we consider commutation relations between the operators
\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
and
\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, which follow from the equations (
4.1
), (
4.2
). In fact, our construction of the operators
\mathbb{T}^{b}_{r}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),\mathbb{T}^{a}_{r}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is motivated by (
4.1
), (
4.2
) and the desire for the arguments below to work, while the linear combinations
\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
,
\mathbb{B}^{*}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
are distinguished by especially nice commutation relations.

Proposition 4.5
.

We have

\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\frac{(a_{1}/y;q)_{\infty}(x/b_{1};q)_{\infty}}{(a_{1}/b_{1};q)_{\infty}(x/y;q)_{\infty}}\,\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),

or, more precisely, the following relation holds:

\sum_{\lambda}\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=\frac{(a_{1}/y;q)_{\infty}(x/b_{1};q)_{\infty}}{(a_{1}/b_{1};q)_{\infty}(x/y;q)_{\infty}}\sum_{\lambda}\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle,

where both sides are viewed as formal power series in
x,y^{-1}
and
q
.

Proof.

We prove the latter identity, fixing
\mu,\nu
from the statement and setting
L_{r}=\nu_{r}-\nu_{r+1}
,
J_{r}=\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}
.

Step 1:

We start by consequently applying (
4.2
) to get a relation between the operators
T^{b,N}_{I,0}
and
T^{a,N}_{K,0}
. Using (
4.2
) we have

\hbox to173.83pt{\vbox to148.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 71.36163pt\lower-53.14551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Comparing with (
4.4
), (
4.5
), the identity of partition functions above is equivalent to

(4.8)

\sum_{\tilde{I},\tilde{K}}\sum_{\lambda}(y/b_{0})^{\lambda_{1}}\langle\nu|\,T^{a,N}_{K,\tilde{K}}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{I,\tilde{I}}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\ R_{a_{N+1},x,y,b_{N+1}}(\tilde{I},0,\tilde{K},0)\\
=\sum_{J,L}\sum_{\lambda}R_{a_{1},x,y,b_{1}}(I,J,K,L)\langle\nu|\,T^{b,N}_{L,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle(y/b_{0})^{\mu_{1}}\langle\lambda|\,T^{a,N}_{J,0}(y\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})|\mu\rangle,

where in the left-hand side the sum is over partitions
\lambda
such that
l(\lambda)\leq l(\mu)+1\leq N
, with the restriction on length coming from Proposition
4.3
, part (3).

From (
4.4
) we have

\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{I,\tilde{I}}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\lambda}|\,T^{b,N-1}_{I,\tilde{I}+\lambda_{N}-\mu_{N}}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\widetilde{\mu}\rangle\ W^{b}_{a_{N+1},x,b_{N}}(\tilde{I}+\lambda_{N}-\mu_{N},\mu_{N},\lambda_{N},\tilde{I}),

where
\widetilde{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{N},\dots,\lambda_{N-2}-\lambda_{N},\lambda_{N-1}-\lambda_{N},0,\dots)
and
\widetilde{\mu}:=(\mu_{1}-\mu_{N},\dots,\mu_{N-2}-\mu_{N},\mu_{N-1}-\mu_{N},0\dots)
. But
N>l(\mu)
, so
\mu_{N}=0
and hence

\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{I,\tilde{I}}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\lambda}|\,T^{b,N-1}_{I,\tilde{I}+\lambda_{N}}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\ W^{b}_{a_{N+1},x,b_{N}}(\tilde{I}+\lambda_{N},0,\lambda_{N},\tilde{I}).

Recall that
W^{b}_{a,x,b}(I,J,K,L)
vanishes unless
L\leq J
, so the expression above vanishes unless
\tilde{I}=0
; hence in the left-hand side of (
4.8
) the nonzero terms have
\tilde{I}=0
. Since
R_{a_{N+1},x,y,b_{N+1}}(0,0,\tilde{K},0)=\delta_{\tilde{K},0}
, the non-zero summands in the left-hand side of (
4.8
) must also have
\tilde{K}=0
and we obtain

(4.9)

\sum_{\lambda}(y/b_{0})^{\lambda_{1}}\langle\nu|\,T^{a,N}_{K,0}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle\langle\lambda|\,T^{b,N}_{I,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\\
=\sum_{J,L}\sum_{\lambda}R_{a_{1},x,y,b_{1}}(I,J,K,L)\langle\nu|\,T^{b,N}_{L,0}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle(y/b_{0})^{\mu_{1}}\langle\lambda|\,T^{a,N}_{J,0}(y\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})|\mu\rangle.

Step 2:

Now we use (
4.9
) to obtain the claim. First, using the definitions of
\mathbb{T}^{b}_{I}
and
\mathbb{T}^{a}_{K}
, we obtain

\sum_{\lambda}b_{0}^{-I}\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{T}^{a}_{K}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{T}^{b}_{I}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\\
=\sum_{J,L}y^{-I}R_{a_{1},x,y,b_{1}}(I,J,K,L)\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{T}^{b}_{L}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\mathbb{T}^{a}_{J}(y\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle,

where in the left-hand side we have used that
\lambda_{1}=\mu_{1}+I
to simplify the term
(y/b_{0})^{\lambda_{1}}
. Both sides are now elements of
\Bbbk[[q,x,y^{-1}]]
,
cf.
Remark
3.4
and part (2) of Proposition
4.3
. So we can multiply both sides by
x^{I}\frac{(a_{1}/x;q)_{I}}{(q;q)_{I}}
and take the sum over
I,K
such that
I+\mu_{1}=K+\nu_{1}
, obtaining an identity in
\Bbbk[[q,x,y^{-1}]]
:

(4.10)

\sum_{\lambda}\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\\
=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I,K,J,L:\\
I-K=L-J=\nu_{1}-\mu_{1}\end{subarray}}(x/y)^{I}\frac{(a_{1}/x;q)_{I}}{(q;q)_{I}}R_{a_{1},x,y,b_{1}}(I,J,K,L)\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{T}^{b}_{L}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\mathbb{T}^{a}_{J}(y\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle.

To prove the claim it is now enough to show that for fixed
J,L\geq 0

(4.11)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I,K\geq 0:\\
I-K=L-J\end{subarray}}(x/y)^{I}\frac{(a/x;q)_{I}}{(q;q)_{I}}R_{a,x,y,b}(I,J,K,L)=\frac{(a/y;q)_{\infty}(x/b;q)_{\infty}}{(a/b;q)_{\infty}(x/y;q)_{\infty}}(x/b)^{L}\frac{(a/x;q)_{L}}{(q;q)_{L}}.

Indeed, if (
4.11
) holds we can apply it to the right-hand side of (
4.10
) to get

(4.12)

\sum_{\lambda}\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\\
=\frac{(a_{1}/y;q)_{\infty}(x/b_{1};q)_{\infty}}{(a_{1}/b_{1};q)_{\infty}(x/y;q)_{\infty}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J,L:\\
L-J=\nu_{1}-\mu_{1}\end{subarray}}(x/b_{1})^{L}\frac{(a_{1}/x;q)_{L}}{(q;q)_{L}}\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{T}^{b}_{L}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\mathbb{T}^{a}_{J}(y\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\\
=\frac{(a_{1}/y;q)_{\infty}(x/b_{1};q)_{\infty}}{(a_{1}/b_{1};q)_{\infty}(x/y;q)_{\infty}}\,\langle\nu|\,\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle.

Step 3:

To finish the proof we need to establish (
4.11
). Since both sides are power series in
x,y^{-1},q
with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}(a,b)
, it is enough to consider the case when
x=q^{S}a
for
S\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
: if for a formal series
f(q,x)\in\mathcal{F}[[q,x]]
we have
f(q,q^{S})=0
for all
S\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
then
f(q,x)=0
,
cf.

[
Ste88
, Lemma 3.2]
. So, from now on we set
x=q^{S}a
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a sufficiently large integer. From (
4.2
) we have

(4.13)

\hbox to154pt{\vbox to131.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 65.95949pt\lower-47.22168pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{40.52167pt}{-14.96132pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{40.52167pt}{22.02733pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-14.96132pt}{-1.70833pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Consider the right-hand side of (
4.13
). Note that for
\tilde{J},\tilde{L}\geq 0
we have

R_{a,q^{S}a,y,b}(S,\tilde{J},0,\tilde{L})=\delta_{S+\tilde{J}=\tilde{L}}\Phi(\tilde{L},0;q^{-S},q^{S}a/b)\Phi(S,0;a/y,q^{-S}y/a)\\
=\delta_{S+\tilde{J}=\tilde{L}}(q^{S}a/b)^{\tilde{L}}\frac{(q^{-S};q)_{\tilde{L}}}{(a/b;q)_{\tilde{L}}}(q^{-S}y/a)^{S}\frac{(a/y;q)_{S}}{(q^{-S};q)_{S}},

where for the first equality we have used the second line of (
3.5
), noting that since
K=0
the only nonzero summand has
P=0
. Since
\tilde{L}=\tilde{J}+S\geq S
and
(q^{-\tilde{L}};q)_{S}
vanishes unless
\tilde{L}\leq S
, we get

R_{a,q^{S}a,y,b}(S,\tilde{J},0,\tilde{L})=\begin{cases}(y/b)^{S}\frac{(a/y;q)_{S}}{(a/b;q)_{S}},\qquad&\text{if}\ \tilde{J}=0,\tilde{L}=S;\\
0,\qquad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Hence in the right-hand side (
4.13
) the configuration around the vertex of type
R
is fixed, and the partition function is equal to

(y/b)^{S}\frac{(a/y;q)_{S}}{(a/b;q)_{S}}W^{a}_{a,y,b}(A,J,0,A+J)W^{b}_{a,q^{S}a,b}(S,A+J,A+J+S-L,L)\\
=(y/b)^{S}\frac{(a/y;q)_{S}}{(a/b;q)_{S}}(y/b)^{A}\frac{(a/y;q)_{A}}{(a/b;q)_{A}}(q^{S}a/b)^{L}\frac{(q^{-S};q)_{L}(q^{S}a/b;q)_{A+J-L}}{(a/b;q)_{A+J}}\frac{(q;q)_{A+J}}{(q;q)_{L}(q;q;)_{A+J-L}}.

For the left-hand side of (
4.13
) we can use (
3.3
),(
3.4
) to write it as

\sum_{I,K}(q^{S}a/b)^{I}\frac{(q^{-S};q)_{I}(q^{S}a/b;q)_{A-I}}{(a/b;q)_{A}}\frac{(q;q)_{A}}{(q;q)_{I}(q;q)_{A-I}}(y/b)^{A+S-I}\frac{(a/y;q)_{A+S-I}}{(a/b;q)_{A+S-I}}R_{a,q^{S}a,y,b}(I,J,K,L).

Note that in the summation above we can assume
I\leq S
, since
(q^{-S};q)_{I}
vanishes otherwise. In particular, the number of terms is bounded by
S
regardless of the value of
A
.

To finish the proof we need to consider the dependence on
A
. Namely, we rewrite (
4.13
) in a way that both sides are rational in
q^{A}
: using the identity

\frac{(u;q)_{A+X}}{(u;q)_{A+Y}}=(uq^{A+Y};q)_{X-Y}

and the expressions above for the both sides we readily obtain

(4.14)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\leq S\\
K=I+J-L\end{subarray}}(q^{S}a/y)^{I}\frac{(q^{-S};q)_{I}}{(q;q)_{I}}\frac{(q^{A-I+1};q)_{I}}{(q^{A+J-L+1};q)_{L}}\frac{(aq^{A}/y;q)_{S-I}}{(aq^{A+J}/b;q)_{S-L}}R_{a,q^{S}a,y,b}(I,J,K,L)\\
=\frac{(a/y;q)_{S}}{(a/b;q)_{S}}(q^{S}a/b)^{L}\frac{(q^{-S};q)_{L}}{(q;q)_{L}}.

Note that the both sides of (
4.14
) are rational functions in
q^{A}
, which are equal when
A
is a sufficiently large integer. Hence (
4.14
) holds for any value of
q^{A}
, and in particular we can set
q^{A}=0
getting

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\leq S\\
K=I+J-L\end{subarray}}(q^{S}a/y)^{I}\frac{(q^{-S};q)_{I}}{(q;q)_{I}}R_{a,q^{S}a,y,b}(I,J,K,L)=\frac{(a/y;q)_{S}}{(a/b;q)_{S}}(q^{S}a/b)^{L}\frac{(q^{-S};q)_{L}}{(q;q)_{L}},

which is exactly (
4.11
) when
x=q^{S}a
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

One can check that (
4.11
) from the proof above is equivalent to the
q
-Gauss identity

\sum_{k\geq 0}\left(\frac{c}{ab}\right)^{k}\frac{(a;q)_{k}(b;q)_{k}}{(c;q)_{k}(q;q)_{k}}=\frac{(c/a;q)_{\infty}(c/b;q)_{\infty}}{(c;q)_{\infty}(c/(ab);q)_{\infty}}.

Proposition 4.7

(
[
BK21
, Proposition 4.5]
)
.

The following relation holds

\mathbb{B}(x_{1}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}(x_{2}\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\mathbb{B}(x_{2}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}(x_{1}\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

Idea of the proof.

This is exactly
[
BK21
, Proposition 4.5]
, and the proof is similar to Proposition
4.5
above, so we provide only a brief sketch of it. First, similarly to steps
1
,
2
of the proof of Proposition
4.5
, we use (
4.1
) to get a commutation relation

\mathbb{T}_{I}(x_{1}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{T}_{J}(x_{2}\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{K,L}W^{b}_{a_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}(I,J,K,L)\mathbb{T}_{K}(x_{2}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{T}_{L}(x_{1}\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

Then the claim follows from multiplying both sides by
(x_{1}/b_{0})^{I}(x_{2}/b_{0})^{J}\frac{(a_{1}/x_{1};q)_{I}(a_{2}/x_{2};q)_{J}}{(q;q)_{I}(q;q)_{J}}
, taking the sum over
I,J
and applying the relation

\sum_{I,J}(x_{1}/b_{0})^{I}(x_{2}/b_{0})^{J}\frac{(a_{1}/x_{1};q)_{I}(a_{2}/x_{2};q)_{J}}{(q;q)_{I}(q;q)_{J}}W^{b}_{a_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}(I,J,K,L)=(x_{2}/b_{0})^{K}\frac{(a_{1}/x_{2};q)_{K}}{(q;q)_{K}}(x_{1}/b_{0})^{L}\frac{(a_{2}/x_{1};q)_{L}}{(q;q)_{L}}

where
K,L
are fixed. The last relation can be proved by taking (
4.1
) and setting
A_{1}=A_{2}=0,B_{1}=L,B_{2}=K
, while
B_{3}\to\infty
and
A_{3}=B_{1}+B_{2}+B_{3}
.
∎

5.
Inhomogeneous spin
q
-Whittaker polynomials

In this section we describe inhomogeneous spin
q
-Whittaker polynomials, originally introduced in
[
BK21
]
, and apply the results from previous sections to establish a new Cauchy-type identity and a new characterization theorem for these functions. We continue to use
\mathcal{A}=(a_{0},a_{1},\dots)
and
\mathcal{B}=(b_{0},b_{1},\dots)
to denote sequences of parameters and we continue to use the notation
\tau\mathcal{A},\overline{\mathcal{A}}
from Section
4
.

5.1.
Basic properties

For a pair of partitions
\lambda,\mu
and a collection of variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
the
inhomogeneous spin
q
-Whittaker polynomial

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is defined in terms of the operators
\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
from Section
4
by

(5.1)

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}(x_{1}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}(x_{2}\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\dots\mathbb{B}(x_{n}\mid\tau^{n-1}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle.

When
\mu=\varnothing
we write
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}
instead of
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\varnothing}
.

The following properties of
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
were proved in
[
BK21
]
;
7
7

7

Our functions
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}
were denoted by
\mathbb{F}^{\sf s}_{\lambda/\mu}
in
[
BK21
]
, and our
a_{r},b_{r}
are equal to
\xi_{r}s_{r}
and
\xi_{r}/s_{r}
from
[
BK21
]
.
for the sake of completeness we sketch their proofs:

Proposition 5.1
.

We have the following expression for the single-variable function
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
:

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\begin{cases}x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|}\displaystyle\prod_{r\geq 1}b_{r-1}^{\mu_{r}-\lambda_{r}}\dfrac{(a_{r}/x;q)_{\lambda_{r}-\mu_{r}}(x/b_{r};q)_{\mu_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}(q;q)_{\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}}}{(q;q)_{\lambda_{r}-\mu_{r}}(q;q)_{\mu_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}(a_{r+1}/b_{r};q)_{\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}}},\qquad&\text{if}\ \mu\prec\lambda;\\
0,\qquad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The vanishing part follows from Proposition
4.3
, part (3). For the explicit expression we use (
3.4
), (
4.4
) and the relation

\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle=(x/b_{0})^{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}\frac{(a_{1}/x;q)_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}}{(q;q)_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}}\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{T}^{b}_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle.

∎

Proposition 5.2
.

The following branching rule holds:

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\nu}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{\mu\prec\lambda}\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{F}_{\mu/\nu}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),

where the sum is over partitions
\mu
such that
\lambda_{r}\geq\mu_{r}\geq\lambda_{r+1}
for all
r
.

Proof.

Follows from (
5.1
) and Proposition
4.3
, part (3).
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=0
unless
\mu\subset\lambda
and
l(\lambda)\leq l(\mu)+n
.∎

Proposition 5.4
.

The functions
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
are symmetric polynomials in
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
.

Proof.

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a polynomial since
\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle
is a polynomial in
x
for any
\lambda,\mu
, and
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a finite sum of products of such matrix elements thanks to Proposition
5.2
. The symmetry in
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
follows from Proposition
4.7
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Using (
4.6
), we can also obtain a graphical definition of the functions
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
in terms of vertex models:

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\geq 0}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}/b_{0})^{r_{i}}\frac{(a_{i}x^{-1}_{i};q)_{r_{i}}}{(q;q)_{r_{i}}}Z^{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}}_{\lambda/\mu}

where
Z^{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}}_{\lambda/\mu}
is the partition function from Figure
2
.

\dots
\dots
\dots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
W^{b}
W^{b}
W^{b}
W^{b}
W^{b}
W^{b}
\cdots
\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3};a_{2},b_{2}
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2};a_{1},b_{1}
r_{n};a_{n},x_{n}
r_{2};a_{2},x_{2}
r_{1};a_{1},x_{1}
\cdots
\mu_{2}-\mu_{3};a_{n+2},b_{2}
\mu_{1}-\mu_{2};a_{n+1},b_{1}
0
\vdots
0
0

Figure 2.
The partition function
Z^{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}}_{\lambda/\mu}
used to compute
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
.

Remark 5.6
.

Spin
q
-Whittaker polynomials were originally introduced in
[
BW17
]
, and later a different but related version was constructed in
[
MP20
]
. The inhomogeneous spin
q
-Whittaker polynomials
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
generalize both these versions: when
a_{1}=a_{2}=\dots=s
and
b_{1}=b_{2}=\dots=s^{-1}
the function
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
degenerates to the corresponding spin
q
-Whittaker polynomial from
[
BW17
]
, while setting
b_{1}=b_{2}=\dots=s^{-1}
,
a_{n+l(\mu)}=0
and
a_{i}=s
for all
i\neq n+l(\mu)
reduces
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
to the version from
[
MP20
]
.

5.2.
Cauchy identity

To formulate the Cauchy identity we define dual functions
\mathbb{F}^{*}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
by

\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\lambda/\mu}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\frac{\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}{\psi_{\mu}(\tau^{m}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),

where
\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is defined in Proposition
4.4
:

\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\prod_{r\geq 0}(b_{r}/a_{r})^{\lambda_{r+1}}\prod_{r\geq 1}\frac{(q;q)_{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}}{(a_{r}/b_{r};q)_{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}}.

Equivalently, the dual functions can be defined using the operators
\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
from Section
4
:

Proposition 5.7
.

We have

\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\lambda/\mu}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})=\langle\mu|\,\mathbb{B}^{*}(y^{-1}_{m}\mid\mathcal{A},\tau^{m-1}\mathcal{B})\dots\mathbb{B}^{*}(y^{-1}_{2}\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}^{*}(y_{1}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\lambda\rangle.

Proof.

Follows immediately from Proposition
4.4
. Note that
\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\psi_{\lambda}(\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})
.
∎

Theorem 5.8
.

For any partitions
\mu,\nu
the following equality of formal power series in
x_{i},y_{j},q
holds:

\sum_{\lambda}\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\lambda/\nu}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})\\
=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(a_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}(x_{i}/b_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}(a_{i}/b_{j};q)_{\infty}}\sum_{\lambda}\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\mu/\lambda}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\tau^{n}\overline{\mathcal{A}})\mathbb{F}_{\nu/\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\tau^{m}\mathcal{B}).

In particular, setting
\mu=\nu=\varnothing
yields

(5.2)

\sum_{\lambda}\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\lambda}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(a_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}(x_{i}/b_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}(a_{i}/b_{j};q)_{\infty}}.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
4.5
in the following way: set

\mathbb{B}^{(n)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):=\mathbb{B}(x_{1}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}(x_{2}\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\dots\mathbb{B}(x_{n}\mid\tau^{n-1}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

Repeatedly using the exchange relation from Proposition
4.5
, shifting
\mathcal{A}
on each step, we have

\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}^{(n)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(a_{i}/y;q)_{\infty}(x_{i}/b_{1};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}/y;q)_{\infty}(a_{i}/b_{1};q)_{\infty}}\,\mathbb{B}^{(n)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\tau\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}^{*}(y\mid\tau^{n}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

Iterating the identity above
m
times, substituting
y=y_{j}^{-1}
for
j=1,\dots,m
, we get

\mathbb{B}^{*}(y_{m}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{A},\tau^{m-1}\mathcal{B})\dots\mathbb{B}^{*}(y_{1}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}^{(n)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\\
=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(a_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}(x_{i}/b_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}(a_{i}/b_{j};q)_{\infty}}\,\mathbb{B}^{(n)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\tau^{m}\mathcal{B})\mathbb{B}^{*}(y_{m}^{-1}\mid\tau^{n}\mathcal{A},\tau^{m-1}\mathcal{B})\dots\mathbb{B}^{*}(y_{1}^{-1}\mid\tau^{n}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

Evaluating both sides of the last identity at
\langle\nu|\cdot|\mu\rangle
yields the claim.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

In the case
b_{0}=b_{1}=b_{2}=\dots=1
Theorem
5.8
was proved in
[
BK21
]
using the dual skew Cauchy identity between spin
q
-Whittaker functions and spin Hall-Littlewood functions.

5.3.
Vanishing and characterization properties

It turns out that the functions
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
satisfy vanishing and characterization properties similar to interpolation symmetric functions. We start with the vanishing property. Recall that
\mathbb{Y}^{n}
denotes the set of partitions of length at most
n
. For a partition
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
set

(5.3)

\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu):=(a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}},a_{2}q^{\mu_{2}-\mu_{3}},\dots,a_{n}q^{\mu_{n}}).

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
be partitions of length at most
n
. Then we have

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}},a_{2}q^{\mu_{2}-\mu_{3}},\dots,a_{n}q^{\mu_{n}}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=0,\qquad\text{unless}\ \lambda\subseteq\mu.

Moreover, when
\lambda=\mu
, we have

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=H_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\neq 0.

where

H_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=(-1)^{\lambda_{1}}q^{\frac{\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}-1)}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}/b_{i-1})^{\lambda_{i}}\displaystyle\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\dfrac{(q^{\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}}a_{j+i}/a_{i};q)_{\lambda_{j+i}-\lambda_{j+i+1}}}{(a_{j+i}/b_{j};q)_{\lambda_{j+i}-\lambda_{j+i+1}}}.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
, with the case
n=0
being trivial.

Assume that we have proved the claim for
n-1
. Using the branching rule from Proposition
5.2
we get

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{\nu\prec\lambda}\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\nu}(a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{F}_{\nu}(\mathbf{x}_{\tau\mathcal{A}}^{n-1}(\widetilde{\mu})\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}),

where
\widetilde{\mu}=(\mu_{2},\mu_{3},\dots)
. Note that by induction hypothesis
\mathbb{F}_{\nu}(\mathbf{x}_{\tau\mathcal{A}}^{n-1}(\widetilde{\mu})\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=0
unless
\nu\subseteq\widetilde{\mu}
. But
\nu\prec\lambda
implies
\widetilde{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\dots)\subseteq\nu
, so if in the sum above the summand corresponding to
\nu
does not vanish then
\widetilde{\lambda}\subseteq\nu\subseteq\widetilde{\mu}
.

At the same time,
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\nu}(a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
vanishes unless
\lambda_{1}-\nu_{1}\leq\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}
, because of the factor
(q^{\mu_{2}-\mu_{1}};q)_{\lambda_{1}-\nu_{1}}
in

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\nu}(a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{B}(a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle\\
=\left(\frac{a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}}{b_{0}}\right)^{\lambda_{1}-\nu_{1}}\frac{(q^{\mu_{2}-\mu_{1}};q)_{\lambda_{1}-\nu_{1}}}{(q;q)_{\lambda_{1}-\nu_{1}}}\langle\lambda|\,\mathbb{T}^{b}_{\lambda_{1}-\nu_{1},0}(a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\,|\mu\rangle.

Hence, if
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\neq 0
, then for some
\nu
we have
\lambda_{1}-\nu_{1}\leq\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}
and
\widetilde{\lambda}\subseteq\nu\subseteq\widetilde{\mu}
. But then

\lambda_{1}\leq\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}+\nu_{1}\leq\mu_{1},

so
\lambda\subseteq\mu
as desired.

To prove the second statement, note that if
\lambda=\mu
then the only possible
\nu
in the discussion above is
\nu=\widetilde{\lambda}=(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\dots)
. Hence

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\widetilde{\lambda}}(a_{1}q^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{F}_{\widetilde{\lambda}}(\mathbf{x}_{\tau\mathcal{A}}^{n-1}(\widetilde{\lambda})\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

and the second statement readily follows from Proposition
5.1
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

We can use the graphical definition of
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
from Remark
5.5
to sketch an alternative proof of Proposition
5.10
. Namely, we have

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\geq 0}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}}/b_{0})^{r_{i}}\frac{(q^{\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i}};q)_{r_{i}}}{(q;q)_{r_{i}}}Z^{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}}_{\lambda},

where
Z^{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}}_{\lambda}
is the partition function from Figure
2
with
x_{i}=a_{i}q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}}
. Note that the factors
(q^{\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i}};q)_{r_{i}}
force
r_{i}\leq\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}
in the sum above. On the other hand, since the weights
W^{b}(I,J,K,L)
vanish unless
I+J=K+L
and
I\leq K
, the partition function
Z^{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}}_{\lambda}
vanishes unless
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}=\lambda_{1}
and
r_{1}+\dots+r_{i}\leq\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{i+1}
for any
i
. These restrictions on
r_{i}
imply that non-zero terms in the sum above must satisfy
\lambda_{i}\leq r_{i}+\dots+r_{n}\leq\mu_{i}
, hence
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
vanishes unless
\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{i}
.

Now we can consider a characterization for
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
in terms of the vanishing property. To do so, we need the following notation. For an integer
n
and a partition
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
define

G_{\mu}^{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{r\geq 1}\frac{(x_{i}/b_{r};q)_{\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}}}{(a_{i}/b_{r};q)_{\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}}}.

Note that
G_{\mu}^{n}
are symmetric polynomials in
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
. Define a filtration
\mathcal{G}^{n}_{0}\subset\mathcal{G}^{n}_{1}\subset\dots\subset\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}
of the algebra of symmetric polynomials in
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
with coefficients in
\Bbbk=\mathbb{Q}(q,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
by

\mathcal{G}^{n}_{m}:=\mathrm{span}\{G_{\mu}^{n}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}\\
|\mu|\leq m\end{subarray}}.

Theorem 5.12
.

For each partition
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
the spin
q
-Whittaker function
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is uniquely characterized by the following properties:

(1)

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\in\mathcal{G}^{n}_{|\lambda|}
.

(2)

For any partition
\mu
such that
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
and
\mu\neq\lambda
we have
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=0
.

(3)

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=H_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, where
H_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is defined in Proposition
5.10
above.

Moreover, for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
both
\{\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}\\
|\lambda|\leq m\end{subarray}}
and
\{G_{\mu}^{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}\\
|\mu|\leq m\end{subarray}}
are bases of
\mathcal{G}^{n}_{m}
.

Proof.

We start with the last statement. Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
and consider the Cauchy identity (
5.2
) with
m=n
and
y_{i}=q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}}/b_{i}
. We get

(5.4)

\sum_{\lambda}\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\lambda}(q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}/b_{1},\dots,q^{\mu_{n}}/b_{n}\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})=\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{(x_{i}/b_{j};q)_{\mu_{j}-\mu_{j+1}}}{(a_{i}/b_{j};q)_{\mu_{j}-\mu_{j+1}}}=G^{n}_{\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

Note that
(q^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}/b_{1},\dots,q^{\mu_{n}}/b_{n})=\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}(\mu)
, so we can apply the vanishing property from Proposition
5.10
to
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}(\mu)\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})
, obtaining

\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}(\mu)\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})=\psi_{\lambda}(\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}(\mu)\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})=0,\qquad\text{unless}\ \lambda\subseteq\mu,

\mathbb{F}^{*}_{\mu}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}(\mu)\mid\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})=\psi_{\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})H_{\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{B}},\overline{\mathcal{A}})\neq 0,

where
\psi_{\lambda}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is defined in Proposition
4.4
. Hence we can rewrite (
5.4
) as

(5.5)

G^{n}_{\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{\lambda\subseteq\mu}c_{\lambda\mu}\ \mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

for some coefficients
c_{\lambda\mu}
such that
c_{\mu\mu}\neq 0
. Thus, the transition matrix expressing
G^{n}_{\mu}
in terms of
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}
is upper-triangular with respect to the partial order of inclusion of Young diagrams. Moreover, this transition matrix has non-zero diagonal entries
c_{\mu\mu}
, so it is invertible and

(5.6)

\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\sum_{\mu\subseteq\lambda}\overline{c}_{\mu\lambda}\ G^{n}_{\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

for some other coefficients
\overline{c}_{\mu\lambda}
.

Note that (
5.5
),(
5.6
) imply that

\mathrm{span}\{\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}\\
|\lambda|\leq m\end{subarray}}=\mathrm{span}\{G_{\mu}^{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}\\
|\mu|\leq m\end{subarray}}=\mathcal{G}_{m}^{n}.

On the other hand, the vanishing property implies that the functions
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}
are linearly independent: if we have

\sum_{\lambda}\alpha_{\lambda}\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=0,

with
\alpha_{\lambda}
being nonzero for some
\lambda
, then we get a contradiction by plugging
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)
for a minimal
\mu
such that
\alpha_{\mu}\neq 0
and using the vanishing property from Proposition
5.10
. Hence
\{\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}\\
|\lambda|\leq m\end{subarray}}
is a basis of
\mathcal{G}^{n}_{m}
, and consequently
\{G_{\mu}^{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}\\
|\mu|\leq m\end{subarray}}
is also a basis of
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{n}
in view of (
5.5
),(
5.6
).

Now we can prove the characterization property. We have already proved that
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\in\mathcal{G}^{n}_{m}
, and the other properties follow from Proposition
5.10
, so we only need to prove that
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is the unique function satisfying properties (1)-(3) above. Let
f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be a function which also satisfies properties (1)-(3), and consider the difference
g(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})-\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
. We have
g(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{G}_{m}^{n}
and
g(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu))=0
for any
\mu
such that
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
. Since
\{\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}\\
|\lambda|\leq m\end{subarray}}
is a basis of
\mathcal{G}_{|\lambda|}^{n}
, for some coefficients
\alpha_{\nu}\in\Bbbk
we have

g(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{\nu:l(\nu)\leq n,|\nu|\leq|\lambda|}\alpha_{\nu}\mathbb{F}_{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

Assume that
g\neq 0
and let
\mu
be a minimal partition such that
\alpha_{\mu}\neq 0
. Then by Proposition
5.10

g(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu))=\alpha_{\mu}\mathbb{F}_{\mu}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\neq 0,

which leads to contradiction. Hence,
g=\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}-f=0
, and the uniqueness follows.
∎

5.4.
Degenerations of
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}

In this section we introduce two degenerations of the functions
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}
, the importance of which is explained in Section
6
where the interpolation symmetric functions are discussed.

We start by considering
b_{i}\to\infty
in the functions
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
. Recall that
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a rational function in
b_{i}
, but Proposition
5.1
implies that when we set
b_{i}^{-1}=0
for all
i\geq 1
, the function just vanishes. So, to get a meaningful object we first need to renormalize the function: set

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\prod_{r=1}^{n}b_{r-1}^{\lambda_{r}-\mu_{r}}\ \mathbb{F}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
denote the result of the substitution
b_{0}^{-1}=b_{1}^{-1}=\dots=0
.

Almost all the properties of
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
described earlier in this section can be readily modified to obtain properties of
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
. For instance, we can compute
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
using degenerations of the explicit expression from Proposition
5.1
and the branching rule from Proposition
5.2
: we have

(5.7)

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\nu}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)=\sum_{\mu\prec\lambda}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\mu/\nu}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\mid\tau\mathcal{A},\infty),

(5.8)

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\mu}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)=\begin{cases}x^{|\lambda|-|\mu|}\displaystyle\prod_{r\geq 1}\dfrac{(a_{r}/x;q)_{\lambda_{r}-\mu_{r}}(q;q)_{\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}}}{(q;q)_{\lambda_{r}-\mu_{r}}(q;q)_{\mu_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}}},\qquad&\text{if}\ \mu\prec\lambda;\\
0,\qquad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In the same manner we can degenerate the vanishing property from Proposition
5.10
, since it does not depend on
\mathcal{B}
in a significant way:

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\lambda,\mu
be partitions of length at most
n
. Then we have

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)=0,\qquad\text{unless}\ \lambda\subseteq\mu,

Moreover, when
\lambda=\mu
, we have

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)=(-1)^{\lambda_{1}}q^{\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{1}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{\lambda_{i}}\displaystyle\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}(q^{\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}}a_{j+i}/a_{i};q)_{\lambda_{j+i}-\lambda_{j+i+1}}.

∎

The only property proved earlier which cannot be immediately degenerated to
\mathcal{B}=\infty
is Theorem
5.12
, since our definition of the filtration
\mathcal{G}^{n}_{m}
makes little sense when
b_{i}^{-1}\equiv 0
. To state the appropriate characterization, let
\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
denote the space of degree
\leq m
symmetric polynomials with coefficients in
\Bbbk=\mathbb{C}(\mathcal{A},q)
, and let
P_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q,0)
denote the
q
-Whittaker function corresponding to
\lambda/\mu
, that is, the
t=0
specialization of the Macdonald symmetric polynomial
P_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q,t)
,
cf.

[
Mac95
, Chapter VI]
.

Proposition 5.14
.

The function
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
can be characterized in the following two equivalent ways: it is the unique function satisfying

(1)

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|}
,

(2)

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)=0
for any partition
\mu
such that
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
and
\mu\neq\lambda
,

(3)

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)=(-1)^{\lambda_{1}}q^{\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{1}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{\lambda_{i}}\displaystyle\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}(q^{\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}}a_{j+i}/a_{i};q)_{\lambda_{j+i}-\lambda_{j+i+1}}
;

and it is also the unique function satisfying

(1’)

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|}
, identically to (1),

(2’)

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)=0
for any partition
\mu
such that
|\mu|<|\lambda|
,

(3’)

The top degree homogeneous component of
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
has degree
|\lambda|
and is equal to
P_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q,0)
.

Proof.

First we show that
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
indeed satisfy the properties from the statement. Properties (2), (2’) and (3) follow directly from Proposition
5.13
. For the remaining properties (1), (1’) and (3’) note that by (
5.8
) the one-variable function
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\mu}(x\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
is a polynomial of degree
|\lambda|-|\mu|
, whose top term coincides with
P_{\lambda/\mu}(x;q,0)
,
cf.

[
Mac95
, Chapter VI, (7.13’)]
. Then (
5.7
) implies that
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
has degree at most
|\lambda|
, and since the branching identical to (
5.7
) holds for the functions
P_{\lambda/\mu}
, we deduce that the top-degree component of
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
is indeed
P_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q,0)
.

To show uniqueness for both characterizations it is enough to prove that if
g\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
and
g(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\mu))=0
for all
|\mu|\leq m
, then
g=0
. Indeed, for the first chracterization fix
\lambda
and assume that
f\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}
satisfies the conditions (1)-(3). Then
g=f-\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
is a polynomial in
\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|}
such that
g(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu))=0
for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n},|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
, since the conditions (2) and (3) fix the values of
f
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
at
\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)
for
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
. Similarly, if
f^{\prime}
is a function satisfying (1’)-(3’) set
g^{\prime}=f^{\prime}-\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
. By (2’) the functions
f^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
both vanish at
\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)
when
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|-1
, and by (3’) the top degree homogeneous components of these functions coincide. Hence
g^{\prime}\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|-1}
and
g^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\mu))=0
for
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|-1
.

So, it is enough to prove that if
g\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
and
g(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\mu))=0
for all
\mu
such that
|\mu|\leq m
, then
g=0
. This follows from Lemma
5.15
below, which for later convenience we state in a much greater generality.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

Assume that for a function
\mho:\mathbb{Y}^{n}\to\Bbbk^{n}
there exists a family of polynomials
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
such that

•

F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|}
;

•

F_{\lambda}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)=0
for any partitions
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
such that
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|,\lambda\neq\mu
;

•

F_{\lambda}(\mho(\lambda)\mid\mho)\neq 0
.

Then the following holds

(1)

The functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
are uniquely determined up to a scalar;

(2)

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. If
f\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
and
f(\mho(\mu))=0
for all
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
such that
|\mu|\leq m
, then
f=0
.

(3)

For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
with
|\lambda|\leq m
form a basis of
\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
;

(4)

The degree of
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
is
|\lambda|
.

Proof.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and fix a choice of functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
for
|\lambda|\leq m
. We claim that these functions are linearly independent: assume that

\sum_{\lambda:|\lambda|\leq m}\alpha_{\lambda}F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)=0

with
\alpha_{\lambda}\neq 0
for some
\lambda
. Choose
\mu
such that
\alpha_{\mu}\neq 0
with minimal possible
|\mu|
. Then for all
\lambda\neq\mu
either
|\lambda|<|\mu|
and
\alpha_{\lambda}=0
, or
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
and
F_{\lambda}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)=0
by the definition of
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
. Hence

\sum_{\lambda:|\lambda|\leq m}\alpha_{\lambda}F_{\lambda}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)=\alpha_{\mu}F_{\mu}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)\neq 0,

leading to contradiction.

Note that the
\Bbbk
-dimension of
\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
is equal to the number of partitions
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
such that
|\lambda|\leq m
, hence the functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
with
|\lambda|\leq m
form a basis of
\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
, proving (3). In particular,
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
with
|\lambda|\leq m-1
form a basis of
\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m-1}
, so if
|\lambda|=m
then the degree of
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
is
m
, proving (4). Finally, (2) implies (1) by considering the difference between two candidates for
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
, so we only need to prove the former. Let
f\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
be such that
f(\mho(\mu))=0
for all
\mu
satisfying
|\mu|\leq m
, and assume that
f\neq 0
. Consider the expansion

f=\sum_{\lambda:|\lambda|\leq m}\alpha_{\lambda}F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho).

and choose
\mu
such that
\alpha_{\mu}\neq 0
with minimal
|\mu|
. Then, in the same way as in the first part of the proof,

f(\mho(\mu))=\sum_{\lambda:|\mu|\leq|\lambda|\leq m}\alpha_{\lambda}F_{\lambda}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)=\alpha_{\mu}F_{\mu}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)\neq 0,

leading to contradiction.
∎

Another degeneration of
\mathbb{F}_{\lambda}
is obtained by considering the regime
q,x_{i},a_{i}\to 1
in
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
. More precisely, let
\varepsilon,d,r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
be parameters such that
d\neq 0
, and let
\mathcal{C}=(c_{0},c_{1},\dots)
be an infinite sequence of real parameters. Define
\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda/\mu}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
as the limit

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda/\mu}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)=(-d)^{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{|\mu|-\mu_{1}-|\lambda|+\lambda_{1}}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda/\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)

in the following regime:

(5.9)

x_{i}=e^{\varepsilon r_{i}},\qquad a_{i}=e^{\varepsilon c_{i}},\qquad q=e^{\varepsilon d}.

From (
5.8
) we get the following expression for the one-variable function

(5.10)

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda/\mu}(r\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{(\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1})!\prod_{j=0}^{\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i}-1}(r-c_{i}-jd)}{(\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i})!(\mu_{i}-\lambda_{i+1})!},\qquad&\text{if}\ \mu\prec\lambda;\\
0,\qquad&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where we have used the following relation

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-k}(e^{\varepsilon u};e^{\varepsilon d})_{k}=(-1)^{k}\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}(u+jd).

From (
5.10
) we see that
\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda/\mu}(r\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
is a polynomial in
r
of degree
|\lambda|-|\mu|
, with coefficients depending polynomially on
d
and
c_{i}
. Moreover, the top homogeneous degree term is
P_{\lambda/\mu}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n};1,0)
, that is, it is a
q=1,t=0
specialization of the Macdonald polynomial. The branching rule (
5.7
) implies that
{\mathbb{F}}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
is a symmetric polynomial in
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
of degree
|\lambda|
with coefficients in
\Bbbk^{el}=\mathbb{Q}(d,\mathcal{C})
, and the top homogeneous degree of
{\mathbb{F}}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
is
P_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n};1,0)
. From
[
Mac95
, VI.4]
it is known that
P_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n};1,0)=e_{\lambda^{\prime}}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
is the elementary symmetric polynomial, where
\lambda^{\prime}
is the partition conjugate to
\lambda
, and the elementary symmetric polynomials are defined by

e_{\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\prod_{i\geq 1}e_{\mu_{i}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),

e_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{k}\leq n}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\dots x_{i_{n}}.

We have the following analogues of the vanishing and characterization properties, obtained as limits of Proposition
5.13
and Proposition
5.14
. For a partition
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
set

(5.11)

\mathbf{r}^{n}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)=(c_{1}+(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})d,c_{2}+(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})d,\dots,c_{n}+\mu_{n}d).

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
\lambda,\mu
be partitions of length at most
n
. Then we have

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}({\mathbf{r}}^{n}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)=0,\qquad\text{unless}\ \lambda\subseteq\mu.

Moreover, when
\lambda=\mu
, we have

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}({\mathbf{r}}_{\mathcal{C}}^{n}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)=(-1)^{|\lambda|}(-d)^{\lambda_{1}}\displaystyle\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{\lambda_{i+j}-\lambda_{i+j+1}}(c_{j+i}-c_{i}+d(\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}+k-1)).

∎

Proof.

Note that in the regime (
5.9
)

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}({\mathbf{r}}^{n}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)=(-d)^{\lambda_{1}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-|\lambda|+\lambda_{1}}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{A},\infty),

so we obtain the claim as the limit of Proposition
5.13
.
∎

Proposition 5.17
.

The function
\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
is the unique function satisfying

(1)

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)\in\Bbbk[r_{1},\dots,r_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|}
,

(2)

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{r}^{n}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)=0
for any partition
\mu
such that
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
and
\mu\neq\lambda
,

(3)

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{r}_{\mathcal{C}}^{n}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)=(-1)^{|\lambda|}(-d)^{\lambda_{1}}\displaystyle\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{\lambda_{i+j}-\lambda_{i+j+1}}(c_{j+i}-c_{i}+d(\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}+k-1))
;

and it is also the unique function satisfying

(1’)

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)\in\Bbbk[r_{1},\dots,r_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|}
, identically to (1),

(2’)

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{r}^{n}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)=0
for any partition
\mu
such that
|\mu|<|\lambda|
,

(3’)

The top degree homogeneous component of
\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
has degree
|\lambda|
and is equal to
e_{\lambda^{\prime}}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
.

Proof.

By the discussion above and Proposition
5.16

\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
satisfies (1)-(3) and (1’)-(3’). The uniqueness follows in the same way as in Theorem
5.14
, by applying Lemma
5.15
with
\mho(\mu)=\mathbf{r}^{n}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)
.
∎

In view of Proposition
5.17
, we call
\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
interpolation elementary polynomials.

Remark 5.18
.

For the later use we note that instead of considering the fields
\Bbbk=\mathbb{Q}(q,\mathcal{A}),\Bbbk^{cl}=\mathbb{Q}(d,\mathcal{C})
we can consider
q,a_{i},d,c_{i}
above as elements of an arbitrary field
\Bbbk
. The induced functions
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}
are well-defined as long as
q
is not a root of unity, and our proofs of the vanishing property and the characterization for
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
hold as long as
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)\neq 0
, which is equivalent to
a_{i}/a_{j}\notin q^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
for any
i,j
such that
i\neq j
. Similarly, the functions
\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)\in\Bbbk[r_{1},\dots,r_{n}]^{S_{n}}
are well-defined as long as the characteristic of
\Bbbk
is
0
, while for the characterization property we also need
c_{i}-c_{j}\notin d\mathbb{Z}
for any
i\neq j
.

6.
Classification of interpolation symmetric polynomials

Our interest in the vanishing property from Proposition
5.13
and the characterization of Proposition
5.14
comes from the fact that the symmetric functions with similar properties were actively studied earlier. More precisely, there are roughly three classes of known symmetric polynomials with similar vanishing and characterization properties: factorial monomial polynomials, factorial Schur polynomials and interpolation Macdonald functions. In
[
Ok97
]
these three classes and their variations were distinguished as the only solutions to a certain interpolation problem. In this section we describe how our new functions fit into this classification, expanding it.

6.1.
Interpolation problem

Below we state the general interpolation problem from
[
Ok97
]
. Let
\Bbbk
be a field; for simplicity we assume that
\mathrm{char}\,\Bbbk=0
. As before, we use
\mathbb{Y}^{n}
to denote the set of partitions of length at most
n
and
\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
to denote the space of symmetric polynomials in
n
variables, of degree
\leq m
and with coefficients in
\Bbbk
.

An
n
-grid
is a map
\mho:\mathbb{Y}^{n}\to\Bbbk^{n}
. An
n
-grid
\mho
is
non-degenerate
if for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
there exists a symmetric polynomial
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
satisfying:

(1)

F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|}
;

(2)

F_{\lambda}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)=0
for every
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
such that
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|,\lambda\neq\mu
;

(3)

F_{\lambda}(\mho(\lambda)\mid\mho)\neq 0
.

An
n
-grid
\mho
is called
perfect
if it is non-degenerate and the polynomials
F_{\lambda}
additionally satisfy the following vanishing property:

(6.1)

F_{\lambda}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)=0,\qquad\text{unless}\ \lambda\subseteq\mu.

The main result of
[
Ok97
]
is the following classification of all perfect grids of a certain form.

Theorem 6.1

(
[
Ok97
]
)
.

Assume that
\mho
is an
n
-grid of the form

\mho(\lambda)=(f_{1}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,f_{n}(\lambda_{n})).

Then
\mho
is perfect if and only if one of the following cases holds:

(
\bf E_{1}
)

f_{i}(j)=c_{j}
where
c_{0},c_{1},\dots
are pairwise distinct elements of
\Bbbk
;

(
\bf E_{2}
)

f_{i}(j)=c_{j-i}
where
\dots,c_{-1},c_{0},c_{1},\dots
are pairwise distinct elements of
\Bbbk
;

(
\bf I
)

f_{i}(j)=a+bq^{j}t^{i}+cq^{-j}t^{i}
where
a,b,c,q,t
are elements of a field extension of
\Bbbk
;

(
\bf II
)

f_{i}(j)=\alpha+\beta j+\beta^{\prime}i+\gamma(\beta j+\beta^{\prime}i)^{2}
, where
\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime},\gamma\in\Bbbk
;

(
\bf III
)

f_{i}(j)=\alpha+\epsilon^{j}{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{i}(\alpha^{\prime}+\beta j+\beta^{\prime}i)
where
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\Bbbk
;

(
\bf IV
)

This case only exists when
n=2
, then
f_{1}(j)=\alpha+\beta q^{j},f_{2}(j)=\alpha+\beta^{\prime}q^{-j}
where
\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime},q\in\Bbbk
.

All cases above should additionally satisfy
f_{i}(j)\neq f_{i^{\prime}}(j^{\prime})
for all integers
i\leq i^{\prime},j>j^{\prime}
.

The polynomials
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
corresponding to the cases
\bf E_{1},E_{2},I
above are respectively factorial monomial polynomials, factorial Schur polynomials and interpolation Macdonald functions. The polynomial
F_{\lambda}
has degree
|\lambda|
, and in these three cases the top-degree homogeneous component of
F_{\lambda}
is respectively a monomial, Schur or Macdonald symmetric polynomial. Moreover, the functions
F_{\lambda}
for case
\bf II
above include interpolation functions related to Jack polynomials, see
[
KS96
]
,
[
OO96b
]
.

Propositions
5.13
-
5.17
for the functions
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
and
\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
indicate that there is another class of solutions to this interpolation problem, where
n
-grids have an alternative form
\mho(\mu)=(f_{1}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}),\dots,f_{n}(\mu_{n}))
,
cf.
the expressions for
\mathbf{x}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mu)
and
\mathbf{r}^{n}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)
from (
5.3
),(
5.11
). In this section we show that the function
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{F}}^{el}
actually lead to all perfect grids of this alternative form, at least when
n\geq 3
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Assume that
n\geq 3
and
\mho
is an
n
-grid of the form

\mho(\lambda)=(f_{1}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}),f_{2}(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}),\dots,f_{n}(\lambda_{n})).

Then
\mho
is perfect if and only if the functions
f_{i}
have one of the following two forms:

(1)

f_{i}(k)=c+a_{i}q^{k}
for constants
c,q,a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\Bbbk
such that
q
is not a root of unity,
q\neq 0
and
a_{i}/a_{j}\neq q^{k}
for any
i\neq j,k\in\mathbb{Z}
. In this case the functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
are proportional to
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\lambda}(x_{1}-c,x_{2}-c,\dots,x_{n}-c\mid\mathcal{A},\infty)
, where
a_{i}
are identified with elements of
\mathcal{A}
.

(2)

f_{i}(k)=c_{i}+kd
for constants
d,c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n}\in\Bbbk
such that
d\neq 0
and
c_{i}-c_{j}\neq kd
for any
i\neq j,k\in\mathbb{Z}
. In this case the functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
are proportional to
\mathbb{F}^{el}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mathcal{C},\infty)
, where
c_{i}
are identified with elements of
\mathcal{C}
.

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
6.2
.

Remark 6.3
.

Following the existing terminology, the characterization property from Proposition
5.14
allows us to call the functions
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}
interpolation
q
-Whittaker polynomials. Note that, while setting
t=0
reduces Macdonald polynomials to
q
-Whittaker polynomials, setting
t=0
in interpolation Macdonald polynomials does not result in any interpolation polynomials, because the characterization property for interpolation Macdonald polynomials does not survive in any form after setting
t=0
.

Remark 6.4
.

Note that Theorem
6.2
does not cover perfect
2
-grids of the form
\mho(\mu)=(f_{1}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}),f_{2}(\mu_{2}))
. While both types of grids listed in Theorem
6.2
are well-defined and perfect when
n=2
, numerical simulations suggest that there exist more general perfect grids when
n=2
. However, our proof of Theorem
6.2
does not cover the
n=2
case.

6.2.
General properties of
n
-grids

We start with adapting some arguments from
[
Ok97
]
to our setting. From now on we always assume that
n
-grids
\mho
have the form

\mho(\lambda)=(\mho(1;\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}),\mho(2;\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}),\dots,\mho(n;\lambda_{n})),

where
\mho(1;\cdot),\dots,\mho(n;\cdot)
are functions
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\to\Bbbk
.

For later convenience, we below restate Lemma
5.15
in terms of non-degenerate grids:

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\mho
be a non-degenerate
n
-grid.

(1)

The functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
are uniquely determined up to a scalar;

(2)

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. If
f\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
and
f(\mho(\mu))=0
for all
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
such that
|\mu|\leq m
, then
f=0
.

(3)

For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
with
|\lambda|\leq m
form a basis of
\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
;

(4)

The degree of
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
is
|\lambda|
.

∎

In particular, given a non-degenerate
n
-grid
\mho
the functions
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
are well-defined up to scalars. We will fix a convenient normalization later.

The following operations on grids will be useful. Let
\mho
be an
n
-grid.

•

For
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
m\leq n
define an
m
-grid
\mho_{m}
by

\mho_{m}(\lambda)=(\mho(1;\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}),\mho(2;\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}),\dots,\mho(m;\lambda_{m})),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{m}.

That is,
\mho_{m}(i;j)=\mho(i;j)
for
i=1,\dots,m
.

•

For
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
l<n
define an
(n-l)
-grid
\prescript{}{l}{\mho}
by

\prescript{}{l}{\mho}(\lambda)=(\mho(l+1;\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}),\mho(l+2;\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}),\dots,\mho(n;\lambda_{n-l})),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n-l}.

That is,
\prescript{}{l}{\mho}(i;j)=\mho(i+l;j)
for
i=1,\dots,n-l
.

•

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
define an
n
-grid
\mho^{k}
by

\mho^{k}(\lambda)=\mho(\lambda+k^{n})=(\mho(1;\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}),\dots,\mho(n-1;\lambda_{n-1}-\lambda_{n}),\mho(n;\lambda_{n}+k)),

where
\lambda+k^{n}
denotes the partition with parts
\lambda_{i}+k
. In other words,
\mho^{k}(i;j)=\mho(i;j)
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
and
\mho^{k}(n,j)=\mho(n;j+k)
.

Our first goal is to show that the three operations above preserve perfect grids. We start with
\mho_{m}
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Assume that
\mho
is a non-degenerate
n
-grid. Then for any
m\leq n
the
m
-grid
\mho_{m}
is non-degenerate with

(6.2)

F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\mid\mho_{m})=F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},\mho(m+1,0),\dots,\mho(n,0)\mid\mho)

for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{m}
. Moreover, if
\mho
is perfect then
\mho_{m}
is perfect as well.

Proof.

For the first statement it is enough to show that the functions

f_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}):=F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},\mho(m+1,0),\dots,\mho(n,0)\mid\mho),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{m},

satisfy the defining properties of
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\mid\mho_{m})
, which readily follows from noticing that for
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{m}

f_{\lambda}(\mho_{m}(\mu))=F_{\lambda}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho).

The last statement follows immediately from the vanishing property for
\mho
and the identity above.
∎

Proposition 6.7
.

For any non-degenerate
n
-grid
\mho
the following statements hold:

(1)

\mho(i;j)\neq\mho(n;0)
for any integer pair
(i,j)\neq(n,0)
;

(2)

\mho^{1}
is non-degenerate and
F_{\lambda}
can be chosen so that

(6.3)

F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho^{1})\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\mho(n;0))=F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho);

(3)

If
\mho
is perfect, then
\mho^{1}
is perfect.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
and consider

f_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}):=F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},\mho(n;0)\mid\mho).

Note that
f_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})
has degree at most
|\lambda|+n
, and for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n-1}
such that
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|+n
we have

f_{\lambda}(\mho_{n-1}(\mu))=F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(\mho(1;\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}),\dots,\mho(n-1;\mu_{n-1}),\mho(n;0)\mid\mho)=F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(\mho(\mu)\mid\mho)=0,

since
\mu\neq\lambda+1^{n}
. Hence, by Lemma
6.5
we have
f_{\lambda}=0
. In other words,
{\left.\kern-1.2ptF_{\lambda+1^{n}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{x_{n}=\mho(n;0)}}=0
, so
x_{n}-\mho(n;0)
divides
F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
. By symmetry,
x_{i}-\mho(n;0)
for
i=1,\dots,n
divide
F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)
and so

(6.4)

F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho)=g_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\mho(n;0))

for some
g_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq|\lambda|}
.

To prove (1) take
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and set
\lambda=(nj,(n-1)j,\dots,2j,j)
. Recall that
F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(\mho(\lambda+1^{n})\mid\mho)\neq 0
, so from (
6.4
) we have

F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(\mho(\lambda+1^{n})\mid\mho)=g_{\lambda}(\mho(\lambda+1^{n}))(\mho(n;j+1)-\mho(n;0))\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(\mho(i;j)-\mho(n;0))\neq 0.

Hence
\mho(i;j)\neq\mho(n;0)
for any
i<n
and
\mho(n;j+1)\neq\mho(n;0)
, which implies (1) since
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
was arbitrary.

To prove (2) note that from (
6.4
) we have for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}

(6.5)

g_{\lambda}(\mho^{1}(\mu))\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\mho^{1}(i;\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1})-\mho(n;0))=F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(\mho^{1}(\mu)\mid\mho)=F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(\mho(\mu+1^{n})\mid\mho).

By (1) we know that
\mho^{1}(i;j)-\mho(n;0)=\mho(i;j+\delta_{i,n})-\mho(n;0)\neq 0
for any
j\geq 0
, hence (
6.5
) implies that
F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(\mho(\mu+1^{n})\mid\mho)=0
if and only if
g_{\lambda}(\mho^{1}(\mu))=0
. In particular, for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}
such that
|\mu|\leq|\lambda|
and
\mu\neq\lambda
we get
g_{\lambda}(\mho^{1}(\mu))=0
, while
g_{\lambda}(\mho^{1}(\lambda))\neq 0
. Since the degree of
F_{\lambda+1^{n}}
is
|\lambda|+n
, the degree of
g_{\lambda}
is not greater than
|\lambda|
. So,
g_{\lambda}
satisfies the defining properties of
F_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\mho^{1})
, which proves (2).

To prove part (3) assume that
\mho
is perfect. Then
F_{\lambda+1^{n}}(\mho(\mu+1^{n})\mid\mho)=0
unless
\lambda\subseteq\mu
, and hence
g_{\lambda}(\mho^{1}(\mu))=F_{\lambda}(\mho^{1}(\mu)\mid\mho^{1})=0
unless
\lambda\subseteq\mu
. So the vanishing property for
\mho
implies the vanishing property for
\mho^{1}
.
∎

Using Proposition
6.7
we can inductively get the following results.

Corollary 6.8
.

If
\mho
is non-degenerate then
\mho^{k}
is non-degenerate for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. If
\mho
is perfect then
\mho^{k}
is perfect for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. ∎

Corollary 6.9
.

If
\mho
is non-degenerate then

\mho(i;j)\neq\mho(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})

for all pairs
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
\mho
be a non-degenerate
n
-grid and fix
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. Without loss of generality we can assume that
i\leq i^{\prime}
. By Proposition
6.6

\mho_{i^{\prime}}
is also non-degenerate, hence replacing
\mho
by
\mho_{i^{\prime}}
we can assume that
i^{\prime}=n
. If
i<n
, then
\mho^{j^{\prime}}
is non-degenerate, and by Proposition
6.7
applied to
\mho^{j^{\prime}}

\mho(i;j)=\mho^{j^{\prime}}(i;j)\neq\mho^{j^{\prime}}(n;0)=\mho(i^{\prime};j^{\prime}).

If
i=i^{\prime}=n
, then we can additionally assume that
j>j^{\prime}
and use Proposition
6.7
to get

\mho(n;j)=\mho^{j^{\prime}}(n;j-j^{\prime})\neq\mho^{j^{\prime}}(n;0)=\mho(n;j^{\prime}).

∎

It turns out that the converse to Corollary
6.9
is also true, so we can classify all non-degenerate grids:

Proposition 6.10
.

An
n
-grid
\mho
is non-degenerate if and only if
\mho(i;j)\neq\mho(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
for all
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.9
we only need to prove that
\mho
is non-degenerate if
\mho(i;j)\neq\mho(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
for all
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. It is enough to prove the following statement: for any
\mho
as in the statement, any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and any function

\phi(\lambda):\{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n}:|\lambda|\leq m\}\to\Bbbk

there exists a polynomial
f\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]^{S_{n}}_{\leq m}
such that
f(\mho(\lambda))=\phi(\lambda)
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{n},|\lambda|\leq m
.

We prove the latter claim by induction on
n
and
m
, reducing the claim for
(n,m)
to the claims for
(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})
with either
n^{\prime}<n
or
m^{\prime}<m
. Note that the cases when
n=1
or
m=0
are trivial.

For the inductive step recall that the monomial symmetric functions
m_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
are defined by

m_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{\alpha}x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}},

where the sum is over all permutations
\alpha
of the
n
